<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove that Catalan's constant $`G=\sum_{j\geq0}(-1)^j/(2j+1)^2`$ is irrational.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): Catalan’s constant is irrational.
  - Explanation: The paper asserts qualitative irrationality of Catalan’s constant.
- **C2** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-rationality)): Each raw entry $F_j(r)$ lies in $\mathbb Q+\mathbb QG$. Consequently, if $G\in\mathbb Q$, then $\Delta_N\in\mathbb Q$ for every positive integer $N$.
  - Explanation: Taylor contact cancels the zeta contribution in each raw entry; rationality of Catalan’s constant then makes the determinants rational.
- **C3** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-two-adic)): With $\delta=(q+g+h)/n=5/24$, one has, for every positive integer $N$, $$\label{eq:two-adic-bound} v_2(\Delta_N)\ge -\left(\frac{\delta}{2}+\frac{\delta^2}{8}\right)n^2 -O_G\bigl(n\log(n+2)\bigr) =-\frac{505}{4608}n^2-O_G\bigl(n\log(n+2)\bigr).$$ The same bound holds for every full minor of the raw column matrix.
  - Explanation: Under the rationality hypothesis, alternating column expansions give a uniform valuation bound at the prime two, also for full raw minors.
- **C4** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-odd-digit-reductions)): For $0\le i,j<H$, let $$\ell=j\bmod p,\quad d=(j-i-1)\bmod p, \qquad i'=\frac{i+1+d-\ell}{p}-1,\quad j'=\left\lfloor\frac jp\right\rfloor, \qquad 0\le\ell,d<p.$$ Then $i'\ge-1$, and, with the convention $M^0(-1,j')=k^+_{j'+1}$, $$\label{eq:digit-layers} \begin{split} p^2M^0(i,j)&\equiv E_dM^0(i',j')\pmod p,\\ p^2Z^0(i,j)&\equiv \begin{cases} Z^0(\lfloor i/p\rfloor,j'),&i\equiv j\pmod p,\\ 0,&i\not\equiv j\pmod p. \end{cases} \end{split}$$
  - Explanation: In the stated large odd-prime range, rational moments reduce by digit extraction, including the negative boundary convention.
- **C5** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-odd-central-layer)): Suppose $H/2<p\le H$, and write $K=L-p$ and $J=H-p$. For every central column, by which we mean $$b\le j<\min(p,L),\qquad j\ge J,$$ one has $$\label{eq:central-layer} pF_j\in\mathbb Z_p^n,\qquad pF_j\equiv-\sum_{0\le\ell<J}\frac{V_\ell}{j-\ell}\pmod p.$$ All denominators $j-\ell$ occurring here are units at $p$.
  - Explanation: Central columns in the specified prime range have scaled residues expressed through the leading vectors of noncentral columns.
- **C6** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-odd-local-elimination)): For $H/2<p\le H$, define $$\begin{aligned} R&=K_++(K-A)_++(J-\max(b,K))_+,\\ S&=(\min(A,K)-b)_+ +(\min(b,J)-\max(0,K))_+. \end{aligned}$$ Then every full raw minor, and hence the filtered determinant, satisfies $$\label{eq:odd-two-layer-bound} v_p(\Delta_N)\ge-\min(2n,n+R,2R+S).$$
  - Explanation: Invertible local column operations control both denominator layers and transfer the resulting bounds to all full minors.
- **C7** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-finite-lower)): Assume $G\in\mathbb Q$. Along any sequence of positive integers $N\to\infty$ for which $\Delta_N\ne0$, one has $$\label{eq:finite-lower-final} \liminf_{N\to\infty} \left(\frac{\log\|\Delta_N\|}{n^2}-\frac12\log2\right) \ge -\frac{8609}{4608} -\left(\frac12+\frac{505}{4608}\right)\log2 >-2.29084.$$
  - Explanation: Under rationality, prime-loss estimates give a lower limit along sequences of nonzero determinants.
- **C8** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-nonvanishing)): Assume that $G\in\mathbb Q$. For every sufficiently large prime $p$, the determinant with scale parameter $N=p$ satisfies $$\label{eq:nonvanishing-exact-valuation} v_p(\Delta_p)=-96p.$$ In particular, $\Delta_p\ne0$ for every sufficiently large prime $p$.
  - Explanation: Under rationality, prime-scale determinants are nonzero for all sufficiently large primes, using fixed rational matrix certificates.
- **C9** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-real-uniform-interpolation)): Let $x_1,\ldots,x_n$ be distinct nonzero real numbers in $(-1,1)$ satisfying Equation <a href="#eq:case-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:case-condition">[eq:case-condition]</a>. There is a function $h_*$ holomorphic on a neighborhood of the closed unit disk such that $$h_*(x_i)=c_i x_i^{D_*},\qquad c_i=\begin{cases}1,&x_i<0,\\-5,&x_i>0,\end{cases} \qquad \sup_{\|z\|\le1}\|h_*(z)\|\le K_0 n, \quad K_0=10e^{12}.$$ The neighborhood may depend on the node list; the displayed bound does not.
  - Explanation: Distinct nonzero interior nodes satisfying the stated sum condition admit a holomorphic interpolant with a uniform norm bound even when nodes cluster.
- **C10** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-first-sheet-bound)): Under Equation <a href="#eq:case-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:case-condition">[eq:case-condition]</a>, $$\label{eq:real-first-sheet-bound} \|\mathcal R_n(x)\|\le(1+K_0n)^n \mathcal V(1/x)\prod_i\|x_i\|^g.$$ In particular, the prefactor is $\exp(O(n\log n))$ uniformly in the nodes.
  - Explanation: Under the sum condition, compression in a finite-dimensional Hardy subspace bounds mixed evaluations without an inverse evaluation estimate.
- **C11** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-second-sheet-bound)): For every distinct nonzero real list in $(-1,1)$, and in particular when Equation <a href="#eq:case-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:case-condition">[eq:case-condition]</a> fails, $$\label{eq:real-second-sheet-bound} \|\mathcal R_n(x)\|\le (3/2)^n n^{n/2}2^{-\binom n2} \prod_i(1+x_i^2)^{(n-1)/2}\|x_i\|^{g-(n-1)}.$$ The factor $(3/2)^n n^{n/2}$ is $\exp(O(n\log n))$; the power $2^{-\binom n2}$ is retained in the principal integrand below.
  - Explanation: Hadamard’s inequality bounds the sheet determinant for every distinct nonzero interior node list, including the complementary case.
- **C12** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-energy-reduction)): Let $p,v$ be two such sequences, and take $\lambda=0$ in case $\kappa=2$ or any fixed $\lambda\ge0$ in case $\kappa=1$. Uniformly over configurations in the indicated case, $$\begin{aligned} &\limsup_{N\to\infty}\ \sup_{\text{case }\kappa} \left(\frac{\log\mathcal I_{\kappa,n}}{n^2} -\frac12\log2\right)\notag\\ &\qquad\le (-1+\alpha+\gamma)\log2 +\kappa\\|p\\|_*^2+\frac12\\|v\\|_*^2\notag\\ &\qquad\quad+\sup_{\substack{-1\le x<1\\x\ne0}} \{W_\kappa(x)+\lambda D(x)-2\kappa T(p,x)-S(v,x)\}\notag\\ &\qquad\quad+\sup_{0<s<1}\{W_s(s)+2T(v,s)\}. \label{eq:energy-dual} \end{aligned}$$ Consequently the maximum of the right sides for the two cases bounds $$\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left(n^{-2}\log\|\Delta_N\|-\tfrac12\log2\right).$$ We use $\log0=-\infty$.
  - Explanation: Exponentially decaying trial sequences and the stated multiplier regime reduce logarithmic interactions to quadratic norms and one-variable suprema.
- **C13** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-real-upper)): For the determinants of Equation <a href="#eq:determinant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:determinant">[eq:determinant]</a>, $$\label{eq:certified-real-upper} \limsup_{N\to\infty}\left(\frac{\log\|\Delta_N\|}{n^2} -\frac12\log2\right) \le -2.290939875<-2.2909.$$ The separate bounds for the majorants in cases $\kappa=2$ and $\kappa=1$ are $-2.290939875$ and $-2.296789875$, respectively.
  - Explanation: Exact trial data, exhaustive stationary-point brackets and value enclosures give the real-place upper limit in both cases.

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-odd-digit-reductions",
        "exact_quote": "For $0\\le i,j<H$, let $$\\ell=j\\bmod p,\\quad d=(j-i-1)\\bmod p,\n \\qquad\n i'=\\frac{i+1+d-\\ell}{p}-1,\\quad j'=\\left\\lfloor\\frac jp\\right\\rfloor,\n \\qquad 0\\le\\ell,d<p.$$ Then $i'\\ge-1$, and, with the convention $M^0(-1,j')=k^+_{j'+1}$, $$\\label{eq:digit-layers}\n \\begin{split}\n p^2M^0(i,j)&\\equiv E_dM^0(i',j')\\pmod p,\\\\\n p^2Z^0(i,j)&\\equiv\n \\begin{cases}\n Z^0(\\lfloor i/p\\rfloor,j'),&i\\equiv j\\pmod p,\\\\\n 0,&i\\not\\equiv j\\pmod p.\n \\end{cases}\n \\end{split}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-005.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "anchor": "stmt-lem-odd-digit-reductions",

          "source_id": "PM-005.1"
        }
      ],

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-odd-central-layer",
        "exact_quote": "Suppose $H/2<p\\le H$, and write $K=L-p$ and $J=H-p$.         "source_id": "PM-005.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "source_id": "PM-005.1"
        }

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-odd-local-elimination",
        "exact_quote": "For $H/2<p\\le H$, define $$\\begin{aligned}\n R&=K_++(K-A)_++(J-\\max(b,K))_+,\\\\\n S&=(\\min(A,K)-b)_+\n        +(\\min(b,J)-\\max(0,K))_+.\n\\end{aligned}$$ Then every full raw minor, and hence the filtered determinant, satisfies $$\\label{eq:odd-two-layer-bound}\n v_p(\\Delta_N)\\ge-\\min(2n,n+R,2R+S).$$",
        "source_id": "PM-005.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "anchor": "stmt-lem-odd-local-elimination",

          "source_id": "PM-005.1"
        }

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-real-uniform-interpolation",
        "exact_quote": "Let $x_1,\\ldots,x_n$ be distinct nonzero real numbers in $(-1,1)$ satisfying Equation <a href=\"#eq:case-condition\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:case-condition\">[eq:case-condition]</a>.         "exact_quote": "Let $p,v$ be two such sequences, and take $\\lambda=0$ in case $\\kappa=2$ or any fixed $\\lambda\\ge0$ in case $\\kappa=1$. Uniformly over configurations in the indicated case, $$\\begin{aligned}\n &\\limsup_{N\\to\\infty}\\ \\sup_{\\text{case }\\kappa}\n \\left(\\frac{\\log\\mathcal I_{\\kappa,n}}{n^2}\n                  -\\frac12\\log2\\right)\\notag\\\\\n &\\qquad\\le (-1+\\alpha+\\gamma)\\log2\n       +\\kappa\\|p\\|_*^2+\\frac12\\|v\\|_*^2\\notag\\\\\n &\\qquad\\quad+\\sup_{\\substack{-1\\le x<1\\\\x\\ne0}}\n       \\{W_\\kappa(x)+\\lambda D(x)-2\\kappa T(p,x)-S(v,x)\\}\\notag\\\\\n &\\qquad\\quad+\\sup_{0<s<1}\\{W_s(s)+2T(v,s)\\}.\n \\label{eq:energy-dual}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Consequently the maximum of the right sides for the two cases bounds $$\\limsup_{N\\to\\infty}\\left(n^{-2}\\log|\\Delta_N|-\\tfrac12\\log2\\right).$$ We use $\\log0=-\\infty$.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Catalan’s constant and Dirichlet beta function** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Catalan’s constant is the real number $$G=\beta(2)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^j}{(2j+1)^2}, \qquad \beta(s)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^j}{(2j+1)^s}\quad(s>0).$$
- **Polynomial rows** ([source](#eq:rows)): For $0\le r<n$, define $$\label{eq:rows} R_r=(1-t)^h t^{C-1}w^{r-g},\qquad P_r=\frac{R_r+R_r^*}{2},\qquad D_r=\frac{t(R_r^*-R_r)}{2f}.$$
- **Mixed moments** ([source](#sec:foundations)): For nonnegative integers $i,j$, define $$\label{eq:mixed-moments} \begin{split} M(i,j)&=\int_{-1}^1\int_0^1 \frac{\|t\|}{f(t)}\frac{t^i s^j}{1-ts}\,ds\,dt,\\ Z(i,j)&=\int_0^1\int_0^1\frac{t^i s^j}{1-ts}\,ds\,dt. \end{split}$$
- **Raw moment entries** ([source](#eq:determinant)): For each raw column index $b\le j<L$, let $$F_j(r)=M(P_r,j)-\frac32 Z(D_r,j)\qquad(0\le r<n).$$
- **Filtered determinant** ([source](#eq:determinant)): The determinant used throughout the proof is $$\label{eq:determinant} \Delta_N=\det_{0\le r,k<n} \left(M\bigl(P_r,s^{b+k}(1-s)^q\bigr) -\frac32Z\bigl(D_r,s^{b+k}(1-s)^q\bigr)\right).$$
- **Rational Z moments** ([source](#eq:rational-z)): Let $B_i^{(d)}=\sum_{k=1}^i k^{-d}$, with $B_0^{(d)}=0$, and put $$\label{eq:rational-z} Z^0(i,j)=\begin{cases} -B_i^{(2)},&i=j,\\[2pt] \displaystyle\frac{B_i^{(1)}-B_j^{(1)}}{i-j},&i\ne j. \end{cases}$$
- **Energy trial norm and tangent series** ([source](#eq:energy-trial-notation)): For a real sequence $u=(u_k)_{k\ge1}$ with $\|u_k\|\le K r^k$ for some $K<\infty$ and $0<r<1$, define $$\label{eq:energy-trial-notation} \\|u\\|_*^2=\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{u_k^2}{k},\qquad T(u,x)=\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{u_kT_k(x)}{k},\qquad S(u,x)=\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{u_kx^k}{k}.$$
- **Certificate barrier functions** ([source](#pm-h-26)): For these choices put $$\begin{aligned} X_\kappa(x)&=W_\kappa(x)+\lambda_\kappa D(x) -2\kappa T(p,x)-S(v,x),\notag\\ Y_\kappa(x)&=\beta\log x+\gamma\log(1-x)+2T(v,x). \label{eq:certificate-barriers} \end{aligned}$$

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R2** [claim](#stmt-prop-rationality) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-rationality): Taylor contact cancels the zeta contribution in each raw entry; rationality of Catalan’s constant then makes the determinants rational. -> C2
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-prop-two-adic) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-two-adic): Under the rationality hypothesis, alternating column expansions give a uniform valuation bound at the prime two, also for full raw minors. -> C3
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-lem-odd-digit-reductions) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-odd-digit-reductions): In the stated large odd-prime range, rational moments reduce by digit extraction, including the negative boundary convention. -> C4
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-lem-odd-central-layer) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-odd-central-layer): Central columns in the specified prime range have scaled residues expressed through the leading vectors of noncentral columns. -> C5
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-lem-odd-local-elimination) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-odd-local-elimination): Invertible local column operations control both denominator layers and transfer the resulting bounds to all full minors. -> C6
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-prop-finite-lower) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-finite-lower): Under rationality, prime-loss estimates give a lower limit along sequences of nonzero determinants. -> C7
- **R8** [claim](#stmt-prop-nonvanishing) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-nonvanishing): Under rationality, prime-scale determinants are nonzero for all sufficiently large primes, using fixed rational matrix certificates. -> C8
- **R9** [claim](#stmt-lem-real-uniform-interpolation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-real-uniform-interpolation): Distinct nonzero interior nodes satisfying the stated sum condition admit a holomorphic interpolant with a uniform norm bound even when nodes cluster. -> C9
- **R10** [claim](#stmt-prop-first-sheet-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-first-sheet-bound): Under the sum condition, compression in a finite-dimensional Hardy subspace bounds mixed evaluations without an inverse evaluation estimate. -> C10
- **R11** [claim](#stmt-prop-second-sheet-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-second-sheet-bound): Hadamard’s inequality bounds the sheet determinant for every distinct nonzero interior node list, including the complementary case. -> C11
- **R12** [claim](#stmt-prop-energy-reduction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-energy-reduction): Exponentially decaying trial sequences and the stated multiplier regime reduce logarithmic interactions to quadratic norms and one-variable suprema. -> C12
- **R13** [claim](#stmt-prop-real-upper) / [proof](#pm-h-29): Exact trial data, exhaustive stationary-point brackets and value enclosures give the real-place upper limit in both cases. -> C13
- **R1** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-real-upper): The paper asserts qualitative irrationality of Catalan’s constant. -> C1

          "anchor": "stmt-prop-two-adic",
          "exact_quote": "With $\\delta=(q+g+h)/n=5/24$, one has, for every positive integer $N$, $$\\label{eq:two-adic-bound}\nv_2(\\Delta_N)\\ge\n-\\left(\\frac{\\delta}{2}+\\frac{\\delta^2}{8}\\right)n^2\n-O_G\\bigl(n\\log(n+2)\\bigr)\n=-\\frac{505}{4608}n^2-O_G\\bigl(n\\log(n+2)\\bigr).$$ The same bound holds for every full minor of the raw column matrix.",
          "source_id": "PM-005.1"
        }

        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-odd-digit-reductions",
          "exact_quote": "For $0\\le i,j<H$, let $$\\ell=j\\bmod p,\\quad d=(j-i-1)\\bmod p,\n \\qquad\n i'=\\frac{i+1+d-\\ell}{p}-1,\\quad j'=\\left\\lfloor\\frac jp\\right\\rfloor,\n \\qquad 0\\le\\ell,d<p.$$ Then $i'\\ge-1$, and, with the convention $M^0(-1,j')=k^+_{j'+1}$, $$\\label{eq:digit-layers}\n \\begin{split}\n p^2M^0(i,j)&\\equiv E_dM^0(i',j')\\pmod p,\\\\\n p^2Z^0(i,j)&\\equiv\n \\begin{cases}\n Z^0(\\lfloor i/p\\rfloor,j'),&i\\equiv j\\pmod p,\\\\\n 0,&i\\not\\equiv j\\pmod p.\n \\end{cases}\n \\end{split}$$",
          "source_id": "PM-005.1"

          "anchor": "stmt-lem-odd-central-layer",
          "exact_quote": "Suppose $H/2<p\\le H$, and write $K=L-p$ and $J=H-p$. For every central column, by which we mean $$b\\le j<\\min(p,L),\\qquad j\\ge J,$$ one has $$\\label{eq:central-layer}\n pF_j\\in\\mathbb Z_p^n,\\qquad\n pF_j\\equiv-\\sum_{0\\le\\ell<J}\\frac{V_\\ell}{j-\\ell}\\pmod p.$$ All denominators $j-\\ell$ occurring here are units at $p$.",
          "source_id": "PM-005.1"
        }

          "anchor": "stmt-lem-odd-local-elimination",
          "exact_quote": "For $H/2<p\\le H$, define $$\\begin{aligned}\n R&=K_++(K-A)_++(J-\\max(b,K))_+,\\\\\n S&=(\\min(A,K)-b)_+\n        +(\\min(b,J)-\\max(0,K))_+.\n\\end{aligned}$$ Then every full raw minor, and hence the filtered determinant, satisfies $$\\label{eq:odd-two-layer-bound}\n v_p(\\Delta_N)\\ge-\\min(2n,n+R,2R+S).$$",

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **loss_counting**: The finite counting formulas give the continuous loss function in the piecewise affine table, with zero continuation for $x\ge65$.
- **loss_area**: The integrated odd-prime loss is evaluated over the interval.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **finite_to_theorem**: Treat the finite loss calculation as a complete irrationality proof. -> The finite integral alone supplies neither nonvanishing nor the independent real-place bound.
- **omit_surplus**: Improve every selected minor by the total number of improved raw columns. -> A selected full minor can omit improved columns because the raw pool has surplus columns.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The complete odd-prime loss function is specified by finite counting formulas and an exact piecewise affine table; its integral has a finite rational certificate.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON object with points (nonnegative rational strings) and area (boolean).
# PRECONDITIONS: At most 256 points, numerators and denominators at most 10^12 in absolute value, positive denominators.
# OUTPUTS: Exact rational loss values and, when requested, exact integrated loss.
# TIME: O(number of points + number of fixed breakpoints) rational operations.
# SPACE: O(number of points) rational values; rational bit lengths depend on input.
# ARITHMETIC: Exact python-flint fmpq arithmetic; no floating point.
# FAILURE: Malformed input or violated precondition raises an exception and exits nonzero.
import json, sys
from flint import fmpq

def rational(s):
    pieces = s.split('/')
    assert len(pieces) in (1, 2)
    a = int(pieces[0]); b = int(pieces[1]) if len(pieces) == 2 else 1
    assert abs(a) <= 10**12 and 0 < b <= 10**12
    return fmpq(a, b)

def positive(y):
    return max(fmpq(0), y)

def loss(x):
    assert x >= 0
    if x >= 65:
        return fmpq(0)
    if x <= fmpq(65, 2):
        pairs = positive(min(x, 59-x, fmpq(19))-max(fmpq(7), 65-2*x))
        return 96-positive(pairs-4)
    K = 59-x; J = 65-x
    R = positive(K)+positive(K-19)+positive(J-max(fmpq(7), K))
    S = positive(min(fmpq(19), K)-7)+positive(min(fmpq(7), J)-max(fmpq(0), K))
    return min(fmpq(96), 48+R, 2*R+S)

def integral():
    cuts = [fmpq(0), fmpq(25), fmpq(29), fmpq(65,2), fmpq(69,2), fmpq(40), fmpq(58), fmpq(59), fmpq(65)]
    total = fmpq(0)
    for a, b in zip(cuts, cuts[1:]):
        total += (b-a)*(loss(a)+loss(b))/2
    return total

payload = json.load(sys.stdin)
assert isinstance(payload, dict) and set(payload) == {'points', 'area'}
assert isinstance(payload['points'], list) and len(payload['points']) <= 256
assert isinstance(payload['area'], bool)
result = {'values': [str(loss(rational(s))) for s in payload['points']]}
if payload['area']:
    result['area'] = str(integral())
print(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True))
```

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L1** (approximation): Convergence of rational quotients alone does not prove irrationality; the required integer linear forms must remain nonzero and tend to zero after denominator clearing. Blocked use: ['Inferring irrationality from decimal digits or quotient convergence alone.']
- **L2** (nonconstructive): The conclusion is qualitative irrationality. An irrationality measure requires additional control of rational approximations. Blocked use: ['Claiming an irrationality measure or a certified approximation rate from the qualitative conclusion.']
- **L3** (hypothesis): The finite-place lower bound assumes rationality of Catalan’s constant and applies along an unbounded sequence of nonzero determinants. Blocked use: ['Applying the rational product-formula lower bound to an arbitrary real determinant.', 'Dropping nonvanishing from the lower-bound argument.']
- **L4** (hypothesis): The prime-scale nonvanishing statement is conditional on rationality and holds for sufficiently large primes; it does not assert nonvanishing at every scale. Blocked use: ['Treating the conditional nonvanishing statement as a certificate for all positive scale parameters.']
- **L5** (formal_scope): The modular certificate proves invertibility of the fixed rational matrices. The growing determinant additionally requires the stated reduction and factorisation argument. Blocked use: ['Promoting a finite modular matrix check to verification of the entire irrationality proof.']
- **L6** (hypothesis): The holomorphic interpolation bound requires distinct nonzero real nodes in the specified interval and the stated case condition. The complementary case uses the Hadamard bound. Blocked use: ['Using the interpolation estimate outside its case condition.']
- **L7** (edge_case): The norm bound for the interpolant is uniform in the nodes, but the exterior holomorphic neighborhood may depend on the node list. Blocked use: ['Assuming a uniform exterior continuation radius from the uniform norm estimate.']
- **L8** (edge_case): Repeated nodes and zero or boundary nodes require the original integral expression or continuity; the factored Laurent expression is not a pointwise definition at every exceptional configuration. Blocked use: ['Evaluating singular Laurent factors independently at a zero or boundary node.']
- **L9** (hypothesis): The energy bound requires the stated exponential decay of the trial sequences and the permitted multiplier sign in each case. Blocked use: ['Using arbitrary nondecaying trial sequences or an inadmissible multiplier.']
- **L10** (approximation): The real-place certificate must exhaust stationary points and bound values throughout root brackets; numerical maximisation or point sampling alone does not establish the global supremum. Blocked use: ['Replacing root exhaustion and bracket bounds by a numerical search.']
- **L11** (hypothesis): Asymptotic constants may depend on the fixed rational value assigned in the contradiction argument, but remain independent of the determinant scale and integration points. Blocked use: ['Reading the asymptotic estimates as explicit finite-scale bounds uniform over all hypothetical rational values.']
- **L12** (counterexample_scope): The arithmetic hyperbolic volume conclusion concerns orientable arithmetic hyperbolic three-orbifolds defined over the stated Gaussian field and the given volume normalization. Blocked use: ['Transferring the volume conclusion to arbitrary defining fields or arbitrary hyperbolic manifolds.']
- **L13** (unknown): ABSENT: a proof-assistant formalisation or a machine-checked proof of all analytic dependencies is not supplied in the provided paper. Blocked use: ['Claiming that finite certificate reproduction establishes a complete formal verification.']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that Catalan's constant $`G=\sum_{j\geq0}(-1)^j/(2j+1)^2`$ is irrational.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>
#### Statements index

| anchor | kind | label | number | first sentence |
|---|---|---|---|---|

| [stmt-prop-rationality](#stmt-prop-rationality) | proposition | `prop:rationality` | 2 | Each raw entry $F_j(r)$ lies in $\mathbb Q+\mathbb QG$. |
| [stmt-prop-two-adic](#stmt-prop-two-adic) | proposition | `prop:two-adic` | 3 | With $\delta=(q+g+h)/n=5/24$, one has, for every positive integer $N$, |
| [stmt-lem-odd-digit-reductions](#stmt-lem-odd-digit-reductions) | lemma | `lem:odd-digit-reductions` | 4 | For |
| [stmt-lem-odd-central-layer](#stmt-lem-odd-central-layer) | lemma | `lem:odd-central-layer` | 5 | Suppose $H/2<p\le H$, and write $K=L-p$ and $J=H-p$. |
| [stmt-lem-odd-local-elimination](#stmt-lem-odd-local-elimination) | lemma | `lem:odd-local-elimination` | 6 | For |

| [stmt-lem-real-uniform-interpolation](#stmt-lem-real-uniform-interpolation) | lemma | `lem:real-uniform-interpolation` | 9 | Let $x_1,\ldots,x_n$ be distinct nonzero real numbers in $(-1,1)$ satisfying Equation [eq:case-condition]. |
| [stmt-prop-first-sheet-bound](#stmt-prop-first-sheet-bound) | proposition | `prop:first-sheet-bound` | 10 | Under Equation [eq:case-condition], |
| [stmt-prop-second-sheet-bound](#stmt-prop-second-sheet-bound) | proposition | `prop:second-sheet-bound` | 11 | For every distinct nonzero real list in $(-1,1)$, and in particular when Equation [eq:case-condition] fails, |
| [stmt-prop-energy-reduction](#stmt-prop-energy-reduction) | proposition | `prop:energy-reduction` | 12 | Let $p,v$ be two such sequences, and take $\lambda=0$ in case $\kappa=2$ or any fixed $\lambda\ge0$ in case $\kappa=1$. |

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Catalan’s constant is the real number $$G=\beta(2)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^j}{(2j+1)^2},
\qquad
\beta(s)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^j}{(2j+1)^s}\quad(s>0).$$ It is the simplest even value of the Dirichlet beta function, or equivalently $L(2,\chi_{-4})$ for the odd quadratic character of conductor $4$. Its arithmetic illustrates a basic difficulty in the study of special values: rapidly converging rational approximations need not be sufficiently accurate after their denominators are cleared. We prove the following statement about this individual value.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *Catalan’s constant is irrational.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Earlier results and the approximation problem

For odd positive integers, the beta values are rational multiples of the corresponding powers of $\pi$. The even values present a different arithmetic problem (Rivoal and Zudilin 2003, Introduction) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[RivoalZudilin2003](#ref-RivoalZudilin2003)]. Rivoal and Zudilin proved that infinitely many even beta values are irrational, and that at least one of $\beta(2),\beta(4),\ldots,\beta(14)$ is irrational (Rivoal and Zudilin 2003, Theorems 1 and 2) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[RivoalZudilin2003](#ref-RivoalZudilin2003)]. Zudilin reduced this finite collection to the six values through $\beta(12)$ (Zudilin 2019, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Zudilin2019](#ref-Zudilin2019)]. Lai and Zhou subsequently reduced it to the five values $\beta(2),\beta(4),\beta(6),\beta(8),\beta(10)$ (Lai and Zhou 2022, Theorem 6.1) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[LaiZhou2022](#ref-LaiZhou2022)]. Fischler obtained stronger quantitative results for irrationality and linear independence in families of Dirichlet $L$-values (Fischler 2020, Theorems 1 and 2) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Fischler2020](#ref-Fischler2020)]. Such family results guarantee irrational members without identifying the particular value $\beta(2)$.

For an individual value, the basic approximation criterion requires nonzero integer linear forms $A_mG-B_m$ that tend to zero. Convergence of $B_m/A_m$ to $G$ alone does not suffice: multiplication by $A_m$ may remove the decay. Apéry’s recurrence constructions for $\zeta(2)$ and $\zeta(3)$ (Apéry 1979) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Apery1979](#ref-Apery1979)], and Beukers’s integral proofs of their irrationality (Beukers 1979) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Beukers1979](#ref-Beukers1979)] succeed because the analytic decay survives the necessary denominator clearing. For Catalan’s constant, Zudilin constructed Apéry-like recurrences, a continued fraction and double-integral representations (Zudilin 2003, Theorems 1–3) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Zudilin2003](#ref-Zudilin2003)]. His discussion following Theorem 1 makes the obstruction explicit: the displayed integer linear forms do not tend to zero, despite the rapid convergence of their rational quotients.

The denominators themselves became an important part of the problem. Rivoal connected Padé approximation with the hypergeometric construction and proved its conjectured denominator bounds (Rivoal 2006, Theorems 1 and 2) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Rivoal2006Catalan](#ref-Rivoal2006Catalan)]. Krattenthaler and Rivoal subsequently gave a more direct hypergeometric proof of these bounds (Krattenthaler and Rivoal 2008, Theorems 1 and 2) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[KrattenthalerRivoal2008Catalan](#ref-KrattenthalerRivoal2008Catalan)]. These results establish arithmetic cancellation that is invisible in a crude estimate of the individual summands. They still leave a denominator cost too large for the small-linear-form criterion. Krattenthaler and Zudilin later identified two apparently different hypergeometric constructions of the same approximants (Krattenthaler and Zudilin 2019, sec. 2 and Theorem 2) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[KrattenthalerZudilin2019](#ref-KrattenthalerZudilin2019)]. Such identities matter arithmetically because different expressions can make different denominator factors visible.

Nesterenko developed effective approximations using half-integer hypergeometric series and double Euler integrals (Nesterenko 2016) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Nesterenko2016Catalan](#ref-Nesterenko2016Catalan)]. Viola announced joint work with Marcovecchio improving an approximation exponent through the Rhin–Viola permutation-group method (Viola 2022) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Viola2022Catalan](#ref-Viola2022Catalan)]; their report gives the exponent $0.6293\ldots$ for its explicit approximants. A recent preprint of Eskandari constructs further explicit rational approximations satisfying $0<|G-p_m/q_m|\le q_m^{-0.62}$ for all sufficiently large $m$ (Eskandari 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Eskandari2026Approximations](#ref-Eskandari2026Approximations)]. These are bounds for particular constructions, not irrationality measures. An error bound with exponent below $1$ does not by itself make the integer forms $q_mG-p_m$ tend to zero.

Results at other characters and other places give useful comparisons. Calegari, Dimitrov and Tang proved the $\mathbb Q$-linear independence of $1$, $\pi^2$ and $L(2,\chi_{-3})$ (Calegari, Dimitrov, and Tang 2024, Theorem A) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[CalegariDimitrovTang2024](#ref-CalegariDimitrovTang2024)]. Their character has conductor $3$, rather than the conductor $4$ of $G$. Their discussion of two Catalan approximation families again identifies the denominator cost as an obstruction for those constructions (Calegari, Dimitrov, and Tang 2024, Remark 11.1.17) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[CalegariDimitrovTang2024](#ref-CalegariDimitrovTang2024)]. Calegari proved irrationality of a $2$-adic analogue $G_2\in\mathbb Q_2$ (Calegari 2005, Theorem 4.2) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Calegari2005](#ref-Calegari2005)]. As explained by Calegari, Dimitrov and Tang (Calegari, Dimitrov, and Tang 2025, sec. 5.2, footnote 2) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[CalegariDimitrovTang2025](#ref-CalegariDimitrovTang2025)], the related real identity contains an additional $\pi^2$ term that is absent in the $2$-adic identity. This illustrates why the $2$-adic result does not settle the real problem. In our construction, cancellation of an additional $\zeta(2)$ term is likewise essential, although the moment identities and the cancellation argument are proved directly below.

Sun (Sun 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Sun2026](#ref-Sun2026)] has also announced a proof of the same qualitative irrationality statement. No result from that preprint is used here.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The determinant strategy

We construct determinants $\Delta_N$ of size $n=48N$ whose entries combine moments of two elementary kernels. Each moment is a rational linear combination of $1$, $G$ and $\zeta(2)$. The polynomial rows are chosen to have high Taylor contact: two prescribed expressions in the rows have identical initial Taylor coefficients. This agreement cancels the $\zeta(2)$ term in every entry. Consequently, the single hypothesis $G\in\mathbb Q$ makes all the determinants rational.

For a nonzero rational number, its ordinary absolute value is determined by its prime valuations. Bounds on the denominators of $\Delta_N$ therefore give a lower bound on $\log|\Delta_N|$. An integral formula for the same determinant gives an upper bound. The contradiction comes from making these bounds incompatible as the size grows. Zudilin’s determinantal criterion gives a useful precedent for this comparison (Zudilin 2017, Proposition 2 and Section 2) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Zudilin2017Determinantal](#ref-Zudilin2017Determinantal)]. There, a positive moment representation produces Hankel determinants with squared-Vandermonde integrals. Positivity supplies nonvanishing, and the denominator and integral estimates are compared on the scale of the square of the matrix size. His discussion of Catalan’s constant explains why the denominator growth of the approximation family considered there still prevents application of the criterion (Zudilin 2017, sec. 6) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Zudilin2017Determinantal](#ref-Zudilin2017Determinantal)]. Our determinant is mixed and signed; its nonvanishing and its real-place estimate require separate arguments.

Three features of the construction make this comparison possible. First, integral Chebyshev rows provide both the Taylor contact and useful divisibility. At the large odd primes relevant to the leading bound, denominators of order $p^2$ and $p$ must both be controlled. The arithmetic estimate follows these two layers separately; cancellation at the first layer alone would not give the required denominator bound.

Second, nonvanishing is arithmetic. Under the hypothesis $G\in\mathbb Q$, when $N=p$ is itself a sufficiently large prime, Frobenius and a pair of polynomial bases reduce the growing matrix to three fixed rational matrices. An exact finite certificate proves their nonvanishing. The reduction then gives nonzero determinants along an unbounded prime sequence, without asserting positivity of the mixed determinant.

Third, the real estimate respects both branches of the rational parametrization used to construct the rows. A bounded holomorphic interpolant controls their mixed evaluations in one range of configurations; a Hadamard bound treats the complementary range. The interpolation estimate is uniform even when nodes approach one another. Its proof uses a finite-dimensional Hardy-space operator, so the evaluation determinant cancels algebraically rather than introducing an inverse-Vandermonde estimate. Andréief’s integration identity (Forrester 2018) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Forrester2018Andreief](#ref-Forrester2018Andreief)], Cauchy’s double alternant (Krattenthaler 1999) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Krattenthaler1999](#ref-Krattenthaler1999)], and the kernel/compression viewpoint of analytic interpolation (Sarason 1967) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Sarason1967](#ref-Sarason1967)] supply the classical context; the specific estimates are proved below.

The resulting Vandermonde products have logarithmic interactions, as in logarithmic potential theory (Saff 2010) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Saff2010](#ref-Saff2010)]. A Chebyshev expansion of the logarithmic kernel (Garoufalidis and Popescu 2013, Lemma 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[GaroufalidisPopescu2013](#ref-GaroufalidisPopescu2013)] reduces the estimate to quadratic sums and two one-variable functions in each case. We regularize their interactions together, control the omitted diagonals and endpoint terms, and only then take a supremum and pass to the limit. Explicit rational trial coefficients finish the argument. Their certificate exhausts all stationary points and bounds entire root brackets, rather than relying on a numerical search for the maxima.

For orientation, the two estimates are stated for the same normalized logarithm <a id="eq:normalized-logarithm"></a>$$\label{eq:normalized-logarithm}
\mathcal L_N=\frac{\log|\Delta_N|}{(48N)^2}-\frac12\log 2.$$ Under the rationality hypothesis, Proposition <a href="#prop:finite-lower" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:finite-lower">7</a> gives $\liminf\mathcal L_N>-2.29084$ along every sequence of nonzero determinants, and Proposition <a href="#prop:nonvanishing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:nonvanishing">8</a> supplies such a sequence with $N$ prime. Independently, Proposition <a href="#prop:real-upper" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:real-upper">13</a> gives $\limsup\mathcal L_N\le-2.290939875<-2.2909$. These inequalities are incompatible. The argument proves qualitative irrationality; a bound for an irrationality measure would require additional control of rational approximations. Section <a href="#sec:conclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:conclusion">8</a> also deduces irrationality of the minimum volume of an orientable complete finite-volume hyperbolic three-manifold with exactly two cusps, and of certain arithmetic hyperbolic volumes.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Organization and conventions

Section <a href="#sec:foundations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:foundations">2</a> constructs the rows and moments, proves cancellation and rationality, and gives the estimate at the prime $2$. Section <a href="#sec:odd-primes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:odd-primes">3</a> treats odd primes and derives the finite-place lower bound. Its only prime-distribution input is the ordinary prime number theorem; the weighted consequence needed here is proved locally. Section <a href="#sec:nonvanishing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:nonvanishing">4</a> establishes nonvanishing at prime scales. Sections <a href="#sec:real-place" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:real-place">5</a> and <a href="#sec:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:energy">6</a> give the uniform real-place and energy estimates. Section <a href="#sec:certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificate">7</a> supplies the rational data certifying the real-place bound, and Section <a href="#sec:conclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:conclusion">8</a> assembles the contradiction.

All logarithms are natural. We use $v_p(p)=1$ and $v_p(0)=+\infty$; for real estimates we put $\log 0=-\infty$. Constants in asymptotic estimates may depend on the fixed rational value hypothetically assigned to $G$, but never on $N$ or on the integration points. The finite certificates are specified by rational data and arithmetic instructions in the text; supplementary programs reproduce them.

<a id="sec:foundations"></a>
### Polynomial rows and mixed moments

We construct the determinant and prove that its entries are rational under $G\in\mathbb Q$. The row design serves two purposes: Taylor contact cancels $\zeta(2)$, while integral Chebyshev coefficients permit the finite-prime estimates. After the moment calculations, we establish the estimate at $2$; odd primes are treated in the next Section.

<a id="eq:rows"></a>
For each positive integer $N$, set <a id="eq:parameters"></a>$$\label{eq:parameters}
\begin{gathered}
n=48N,\qquad a=11N,\qquad b=7N,\qquad q=g=4N,\qquad h=2N,\\
L=n+a=n+b+q=59N,\qquad C=L+g=63N,\\
H=C+h=65N,\qquad A=a+2g=19N.
\end{gathered}$$ Write $f(t)=\sqrt{1-t^2}$, with the positive branch on $(-1,1)$, and $w=t/(1+f)$. Then $$t=\frac{2w}{1+w^2},\qquad f=\frac{1-w^2}{1+w^2}.$$ The involution denoted by a star sends $w$ to $w^{-1}$, fixes $t$, and sends $f$ to $-f$. For $0\le r<n$, define $$\label{eq:rows}
R_r=(1-t)^h t^{C-1}w^{r-g},\qquad
P_r=\frac{R_r+R_r^*}{2},\qquad
D_r=\frac{t(R_r^*-R_r)}{2f}.$$ Here and below identities involving $f$ near zero use its Taylor branch with $f(0)=1$.

<a id="eq:contact"></a>
Let $T_d,U_d$ be the Chebyshev polynomials, normalized by $T_0=1,T_1=x,U_{-1}=0,U_0=1$, and the recurrence $S_{d+1}=2xS_d-S_{d-1}$. The associated Laurent expressions are the standard formulas (National Institute of Standards and Technology n.d., Equations (18.5.1)–(18.5.2)). Since $(w+w^{-1})/2=1/t$ and $(w^{-1}-w)/2=f/t$, Equation <a href="#eq:rows" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rows">[eq:rows]</a> becomes, with $d=|r-g|$, $$P_r=(1-t)^h t^{C-1}T_d(1/t),\qquad
D_r=\operatorname{sgn}(r-g)(1-t)^h t^{C-1}U_{d-1}(1/t).$$ In particular these are integer polynomials; the second is zero when $r=g$. The inequality $d\le n-1-g$ gives <a id="eq:row-support"></a>$$\label{eq:row-support}
\operatorname{supp}P_r\subseteq\{A,\ldots,H-1\},\qquad
\operatorname{supp}D_r\subseteq\{A+1,\ldots,H-1\}.$$ Moreover $w=t/2+O(t^3)$, so that $$\label{eq:contact}
\frac{tP_r}{f}-D_r=\frac{tR_r}{f}=O(t^{C+r-g})=O(t^L).$$

For nonnegative integers $i,j$, define <a id="eq:mixed-moments"></a>$$\label{eq:mixed-moments}
\begin{split}
M(i,j)&=\int_{-1}^1\int_0^1
 \frac{|t|}{f(t)}\frac{t^i s^j}{1-ts}\,ds\,dt,\\
Z(i,j)&=\int_0^1\int_0^1\frac{t^i s^j}{1-ts}\,ds\,dt.
\end{split}$$ Extend these expressions bilinearly to polynomial arguments. The integrals converge absolutely. Indeed, for $0<u<1$, $$\int_0^1\frac{ds}{1-us}=\frac{-\log(1-u)}{u},$$ and $(-\log(1-u))/\sqrt{1-u^2}$ is integrable on $(0,1)$. This dominates the absolute $M$ integrand after integration in $s$; the corresponding assertion for $Z$ is weaker. Bounded polynomial factors preserve this domination. It also justifies termwise integration of the geometric series used below.

<a id="eq:determinant"></a>
For each raw column index $b\le j<L$, let $$F_j(r)=M(P_r,j)-\frac32 Z(D_r,j)\qquad(0\le r<n).$$ The determinant used throughout the proof is $$\label{eq:determinant}
\Delta_N=\det_{0\le r,k<n}
\left(M\bigl(P_r,s^{b+k}(1-s)^q\bigr)
      -\frac32Z\bigl(D_r,s^{b+k}(1-s)^q\bigr)\right).$$ Thus, if $\mathcal F$ is the $n$ by $n+q$ matrix with columns $F_j$, the matrix in Equation <a href="#eq:determinant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:determinant">[eq:determinant]</a> is $\mathcal F\mathcal T$, where the integer matrix $\mathcal T$ has entries <a id="eq:integer-filter"></a>$$\label{eq:integer-filter}
\mathcal T_{j,k}=(-1)^{j-b-k}\binom{q}{j-b-k},
\qquad b\le j<L,\quad 0\le k<n.$$ A binomial coefficient outside its usual range is zero. In particular, all raw columns required by the filter lie in the contact range $j<L$.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Moment evaluation and cancellation

<a id="eq:moment-values"></a>
<a id="eq:moment-series"></a>
Set $$\label{eq:moment-values}
c_l=4^{-l}\binom{2l}{l}\quad(l\ge0),\qquad
m_i=\int_{-1}^1t^i\frac{|t|}{f(t)}\,dt
=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{2}{(i+1)c_{i/2}},&i\ge0\text{ even},\\
0,&i\ge0\text{ odd},\end{cases}$$ and use the convention $m_{-1}=0$. The moment formula follows from symmetry, $m_0=2$, and integration by parts, which gives $(i+1)m_i=i m_{i-2}$ for $i\ge1$. Throughout, $c_z$ means zero unless $z$ is a nonnegative integer. Termwise integration gives $$\label{eq:moment-series}
M(i,j)=\sum_{u\ge0}\frac{m_{i+u}}{j+u+1},\qquad
M(i,j)-M(i+1,j+1)=\frac{m_i}{j+1}.$$

<a id="eq:boundary-recurrences"></a>
Put $K^-_d=M(d,0)$ and $K^+_d=M(0,d)$. Their boundary recurrences are $$\label{eq:boundary-recurrences}
\begin{aligned}
dK^-_d&=(d-1)K^-_{d-2}+m_{d-2}+m_{d-1} &&(d\ge2),\\
K^-_1&=2,\\
(d-1)K^+_d&=(d-2)K^+_{d-2}+\frac{2}{d-1} &&(d\ge2).
\end{aligned}$$ For the first recurrence, the moment recurrence gives the termwise identity $$\frac{d m_{d+u}-(d-1)m_{d+u-2}}{u+1}
=m_{d+u-2}-m_{d+u}.$$ The right side telescopes, since $m_v\to0$ as $v\to\infty$. The same identity for $d=1$, with the term multiplied by $d-1$ omitted, gives $K^-_1=m_{-1}+m_0=2$. For the last recurrence, shifting the first series by two leaves, for $u\ge2$, the terms $$\frac{(d-1)m_{u-2}-(d-2)m_u}{d+u-1}=m_{u-2}-m_u.$$ Their sum is $2$; the remaining boundary term is $-2(d-2)/(d-1)$, giving the claimed result.

<a id="eq:boundary-starts"></a>
The two independent starting values are $$\label{eq:boundary-starts}
K^-_0=K^+_0=4G,\qquad K^+_1=\frac{\pi^2}{4}=\frac32\zeta(2).$$ To verify the first, integrate in $s$ and put $t=\sin\theta$ on the positive half interval. This gives $$K^-_0=\int_0^{\pi/2}\log\frac{1+\sin\theta}{1-\sin\theta}\,d\theta
=-4\int_0^{\pi/4}\log\tan v\,dv=4G.$$ The last equality follows on setting $x=\tan v$ and integrating the geometric series for $(1+x^2)^{-1}$ against $-\log x$. For the other start, Equation <a href="#eq:moment-series" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:moment-series">[eq:moment-series]</a> and Equation <a href="#eq:moment-values" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:moment-values">[eq:moment-values]</a> yield $$K^+_1=\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{1}{2k^2c_k}.$$ The differential equation $(1-x^2)y''-xy'=2$, with $y(0)=y'(0)=0$, determines the Taylor series of $y=(\arcsin x)^2$ as $\sum_{k\ge1}x^{2k}/(2k^2c_k)$. Its nonnegative coefficients allow passage to $x=1$ by monotone convergence, giving $\pi^2/4$. For completeness, integrating $$\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{\rho^k\sin(kx)}{k}
=\operatorname{Im}\bigl(-\log(1-\rho e^{ix})\bigr),\qquad0<\rho<1,$$ over $0<x<\pi$ and letting $\rho\uparrow1$ gives $2\sum_{k\text{ odd}}k^{-2}=\pi^2/4$. Here the imaginary part is uniformly bounded and tends to $(\pi-x)/2$, so dominated convergence applies. The odd sum is $3\zeta(2)/4$, establishing the final equality in Equation <a href="#eq:boundary-starts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:boundary-starts">[eq:boundary-starts]</a>.

<a id="eq:rational-z"></a>
Define $M^0(i,j)$ by the rational recurrences <a href="#eq:moment-series" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:moment-series">[eq:moment-series]</a>–<a href="#eq:boundary-recurrences" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:boundary-recurrences">[eq:boundary-recurrences]</a>, replacing only the starts $K^-_0=K^+_0$ and $K^+_1$ by zero. More explicitly, let $k^-_d,k^+_d$ satisfy Equation <a href="#eq:boundary-recurrences" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:boundary-recurrences">[eq:boundary-recurrences]</a> with <a id="eq:rational-starts"></a>$$\label{eq:rational-starts}
k^-_0=k^+_0=k^+_1=0,\qquad k^-_1=2.$$ Then the following formulas specify the whole array without ambiguity: <a id="eq:diagonal-reduction"></a>$$\label{eq:diagonal-reduction}
M^0(i,j)=
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle k^-_{i-j}-\sum_{k=1}^{j}\frac{m_{i-j+k-1}}{k},&i\ge j,\\[6pt]
\displaystyle k^+_{j-i}-\sum_{k=j-i+1}^{j}
                  \frac{m_{k-(j-i)-1}}{k},&i<j.
\end{cases}$$ Empty sums are zero. We shall also use the algebraic convention <a id="eq:negative-boundary"></a>$$\label{eq:negative-boundary}
M^0(-1,j)=k^+_{j+1}\qquad(j\ge0);$$ this does not define an additional integral. Let $B_i^{(d)}=\sum_{k=1}^i k^{-d}$, with $B_0^{(d)}=0$, and put $$\label{eq:rational-z}
Z^0(i,j)=\begin{cases}
-B_i^{(2)},&i=j,\\[2pt]
\displaystyle\frac{B_i^{(1)}-B_j^{(1)}}{i-j},&i\ne j.
\end{cases}$$ The evaluations of the moments are <a id="eq:rational-decomposition"></a>$$\label{eq:rational-decomposition}
\begin{split}
M(i,j)&=M^0(i,j)+4G c_{(i-j)/2}
                      +\frac32\zeta(2)c_{(j-i-1)/2},\\
Z(i,j)&=Z^0(i,j)+[i=j]\zeta(2).
\end{split}$$ Indeed, the homogeneous parts of the minus and plus boundary recurrences propagate the starts by precisely these two $c$-kernels. Diagonal reduction preserves both index differences. The formula for $Z$ follows from $$Z(i,j)=\sum_{u\ge0}\frac{1}{(i+u+1)(j+u+1)}$$ by partial fractions when $i\ne j$, and directly when $i=j$.

<a id="eq:h-star"></a>
For later valuation calculations, it is useful to solve the rational boundary recurrences explicitly. Define $$\label{eq:h-star}
H_z^*=\begin{cases}
c_{z/2},&z\ge0\text{ even},\\
\displaystyle\frac{1}{z c_{(z-1)/2}},&z\ge1\text{ odd}.
\end{cases}
\qquad\frac{H_{z+2}^*}{H_z^*}=\frac{z+1}{z+2}.$$ Then <a id="eq:explicit-boundary-arrays"></a>$$\label{eq:explicit-boundary-arrays}
\begin{split}
k^-_u&=\begin{cases}
\displaystyle H_u^*\sum_{\substack{1\le z\le u\\z\ \text{even}}}
            \frac{2}{z^2(H_z^*)^2},&u\text{ even},\\[6pt]
\displaystyle H_u^*\sum_{\substack{1\le z\le u\\z\ \text{odd}}}
            \frac{2}{z},&u\text{ odd},
\end{cases}\\
k^+_{u+1}&=H_u^*\sum_{\substack{1\le z\le u\\z\equiv u\ (2)}}
                 \frac{2}{z^2H_z^*}\qquad(u\ge0).
\end{split}$$ To see this, divide each recurrence by the appropriate $H^*$ and use the ratio in Equation <a href="#eq:h-star" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:h-star">[eq:h-star]</a>. For the minus recurrence the inhomogeneous increment after division is $2/(z^2(H_z^*)^2)$ when $z$ is even and $2/z$ when $z$ is odd. The odd case starts with $k^-_1/H_1^*=2$. For the plus recurrence the increment is $2/(z^2H_z^*)$, with initial values $k^+_1=0$ and $k^+_2=2$. This proves all the formulas, including their empty-sum cases.

<a id="stmt-prop-rationality"></a>
<div id="prop:rationality" class="proposition">

**Proposition 2**. *Each raw entry $F_j(r)$ lies in $\mathbb Q+\mathbb QG$. Consequently, if $G\in\mathbb Q$, then $\Delta_N\in\mathbb Q$ for every positive integer $N$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-rationality"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-rationality</summary>

*Proof.* Since $f(t)^{-1}=\sum_{l\ge0}c_l t^{2l}$, the coefficient of $\zeta(2)$ in $F_j(r)$ is $$\frac32\left(\sum_i[t^i]P_r\,c_{(j-i-1)/2}-[t^j]D_r\right)
=\frac32[t^j]\left(\frac{tP_r}{f}-D_r\right)=0$$ by Equation <a href="#eq:contact" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact">[eq:contact]</a> and $j<L$. More explicitly, <a id="eq:rational-raw-entry"></a>$$\label{eq:rational-raw-entry}
F_j(r)=\sum_i[t^i]P_r
       \left(M^0(i,j)+4G c_{(i-j)/2}\right)
       -\frac32\sum_i[t^i]D_r Z^0(i,j).$$ The assertion follows from the rational arrays and the integer filter. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### A uniform estimate at the prime two

We normalize $v_p(p)=1$ and set $v_p(0)=+\infty$. In the rest of this section assume $G\in\mathbb Q$, viewed also in $\mathbb Q_2$. Constants allowed to depend on this fixed rational number will carry a subscript $G$.

<a id="stmt-prop-two-adic"></a>
<div id="prop:two-adic" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:two-adic-bound"></a>
**Proposition 3**. *With $\delta=(q+g+h)/n=5/24$, one has, for every positive integer $N$, $$\label{eq:two-adic-bound}
v_2(\Delta_N)\ge
-\left(\frac{\delta}{2}+\frac{\delta^2}{8}\right)n^2
-O_G\bigl(n\log(n+2)\bigr)
=-\frac{505}{4608}n^2-O_G\bigl(n\log(n+2)\bigr).$$ The same bound holds for every full minor of the raw column matrix.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-two-adic"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-two-adic</summary>

<a id="eq:two-adic-smoothing"></a>
<a id="eq:smoothing-tail-bound"></a>
*Proof.* We first construct a $2$-adic version of the geometric moment series: $$\label{eq:two-adic-smoothing}
M_{\rm s}(i,j)=\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{m_{i+k}}{j+k+1}\quad\text{in }\mathbb Q_2.$$ For even $u$, the factorial formula implies $$v_2(m_u)=1+u-v_2\binom{u}{u/2}
\ge u+1-\log_2(u+1).$$ The last bound follows, for example, by subtracting the factorial-floor formulas: each binary place contributes at most one to the binomial valuation. Odd moments are zero. If $0\le i,j<H$, every nonzero term of Equation <a href="#eq:two-adic-smoothing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:two-adic-smoothing">[eq:two-adic-smoothing]</a> therefore has valuation at least $$\label{eq:smoothing-tail-bound}
i+k+1-2\log_2(H+k)
\ge i+1-2\log_2 H+k-2\log_2(k+1)
\ge i-2\log_2 H-1.$$ The middle expression tends to infinity with $k$. Thus the infinite tail converges, and the final bound is uniform over all its terms and over $0\le i,j<H$. In particular $v_2(M_{\rm s}(i,j))\ge i-2\log_2H-1$.

<a id="eq:two-adic-column-types"></a>
The diagonal recurrence and both boundary recurrences hold for $M_{\rm s}$ as well. The same finite telescoping calculations prove this because their tails tend to zero $2$-adically. In particular its minus start at one is forced to be $2$. There are therefore exactly two homogeneous discrepancies between $M_{\rm s}$ and the rational part $M^0(i,j)+4G c_{(i-j)/2}$. Write <a id="eq:two-adic-discrepancies"></a>$$\label{eq:two-adic-discrepancies}
e_1=4G-M_{\rm s}(0,0),\qquad e_2=-M_{\rm s}(0,1).$$ These are fixed elements of $\mathbb Q_2$, independent of $N$ and of all degree indices, and $$M^0(i,j)+4G c_{(i-j)/2}
=M_{\rm s}(i,j)+e_1c_{(i-j)/2}+e_2c_{(j-i-1)/2}.$$ Contracting the last kernel with $P_r$ and applying Equation <a href="#eq:contact" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact">[eq:contact]</a> expresses each raw column as the sum of three column vectors: $$\label{eq:two-adic-column-types}
\begin{split}
F_j(r)&=S_j(r)+E_j(r)+B_j(r),\\
S_j(r)&=M_{\rm s}(P_r,j),\\
E_j(r)&=e_1\sum_i[t^i]P_r\,c_{(i-j)/2},\\
B_j(r)&=\sum_i[t^i]D_r\left(e_2[i=j]-\frac32Z^0(i,j)\right).
\end{split}$$ If either discrepancy is zero, the corresponding summands vanish; there is no need to assign a finite valuation to a zero constant.

<a id="eq:coefficient-valuation"></a>
Here are the coefficient and denominator bounds needed for these columns. Remove the factor $(1-t)^h$ and write the resulting polynomials as <a id="eq:unconvolved-coefficients"></a>$$\label{eq:unconvolved-coefficients}
\frac{P_r(t)}{(1-t)^h}=\sum_{u\ge0}p_{r,u}t^{C-1-u},\qquad
\frac{D_r(t)}{(1-t)^h}=\sum_{u\ge0}d_{r,u}t^{C-1-u}.$$ All sums here are finite. Induction in the Chebyshev recurrence shows that the coefficient of $x^u$ in $T_d$ has valuation at least $u-1$ and that in $U_d$ has valuation at least $u$; the assertion at $u=0$ uses only integrality. Hence $$\label{eq:coefficient-valuation}
v_2(p_{r,u})\ge u-1,\qquad v_2(d_{r,u})\ge u-1.$$ Convolution with $(1-t)^h$ uses integer coefficients. Combining Equations <a href="#eq:smoothing-tail-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smoothing-tail-bound">[eq:smoothing-tail-bound]</a> and <a href="#eq:coefficient-valuation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coefficient-valuation">[eq:coefficient-valuation]</a>, every entry of $S_j$ has valuation at least $C-2\log_2H-3$.

Set $L_2=\lfloor\log_2H\rfloor$. Equation <a href="#eq:rational-z" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rational-z">[eq:rational-z]</a> gives <a id="eq:harmonic-two-adic"></a>$$\label{eq:harmonic-two-adic}
v_2(Z^0(i,j))\ge-2L_2\qquad(0\le i,j<H).$$ Indeed, the harmonic sum of order $d$ has valuation at least $-dL_2$; when $i\ne j$, division by $i-j$ loses at most another $L_2$. The ultrametric inequality introduces no loss for the number of terms in these sums or in polynomial convolutions.

<a id="eq:exceptional-scalar-bounds"></a>
Choose a fixed nonnegative integer $K_G$ with $v_2(e_1),v_2(e_2)\ge-K_G$. Let $\mathbf p_u=(p_{r,u})_r$ and $\mathbf d_u=(d_{r,u})_r$. The exceptional columns in Equation <a href="#eq:two-adic-column-types" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:two-adic-column-types">[eq:two-adic-column-types]</a> have expansions $$E_j=\sum_u\mathbf p_u\,a_{u,j},\qquad
B_j=\sum_u\mathbf d_u\,b_{u,j},$$ where $$\begin{aligned}
a_{u,j}&=e_1\sum_{v=0}^h(-1)^v\binom hv
                    c_{(C-1-u+v-j)/2},\\
b_{u,j}&=\sum_{v=0}^h(-1)^v\binom hv
 \left(e_2[C-1-u+v=j]-\frac32Z^0(C-1-u+v,j)\right).
\end{aligned}$$ Only $u$ with a nonzero coefficient vector need be included, so all moment indices in these expressions are between $0$ and $H-1$. Since $v_2(c_l)\ge-2l$, every contributing summand yields $$\label{eq:exceptional-scalar-bounds}
v_2(a_{u,j})\ge u+j-H+1-K_G,\qquad
v_2(b_{u,j})\ge-2L_2-1-K_G.$$

We now apply the bounds in the order needed to preserve alternation. First expand $\det(\mathcal F\mathcal T)$ by Cauchy–Binet. Each term is an integer times a full raw minor, whose column indices $j_1,\ldots,j_n$ are distinct. Fix such a minor, expand each column by Equation <a href="#eq:two-adic-column-types" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:two-adic-column-types">[eq:two-adic-column-types]</a>, and consider a term containing $m$ columns of type $E$, $l$ of type $B$, and $n-m-l$ columns of type $S$.

Expand the $E$ columns through the fixed vectors $\mathbf p_u$ and the $B$ columns through the fixed vectors $\mathbf d_u$. Repeated indices within the first group give equal coefficient vectors and hence zero determinants; the same holds within the second group. No distinctness between the two groups is asserted or needed. For nonzero terms we therefore have $$\sum_{E\text{ columns}}u\ge\frac{m(m-1)}2,\qquad
\sum_{B\text{ columns}}u\ge\frac{l(l-1)}2.$$ The already fixed, distinct raw column indices also give $$\sum_{E\text{ columns}}j\ge mb+\frac{m(m-1)}2.$$ Equations <a href="#eq:coefficient-valuation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coefficient-valuation">[eq:coefficient-valuation]</a> and <a href="#eq:exceptional-scalar-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exceptional-scalar-bounds">[eq:exceptional-scalar-bounds]</a> show that the first group contributes at least $2\sum u+\sum j-Hm-K_Gm$. The second contributes at least $\sum u-O_G(l\log(H+2))$; the smoothed columns contribute at least $C(n-m-l)-O((n-m-l)\log(H+2))$. Thus every term, every raw minor, and finally the filtered determinant satisfies <a id="eq:two-adic-quadratic"></a>$$\label{eq:two-adic-quadratic}
\begin{split}
v_2(\Delta_N)\ge{}&-O_G(n\log(n+2))\\
&+\min_{\substack{m,l\ge0\\m+l\le n}}
\left\{-(H-b)m+\frac32m^2+\frac12l^2+C(n-m-l)\right\}.
\end{split}$$ Finite summation causes no further loss in valuation.

For completeness minimize over real $m,l$; this can only lower the minimum over integer choices. Since $C\ge n$, the expression decreases as $l$ increases up to $n-m$, so its minimum occurs at $l=n-m$. Using $H-b=n(1+\delta)$, the remaining expression is $$\frac{n^2}{2}-(2+\delta)nm+2m^2
=2\left(m-\frac{(2+\delta)n}{4}\right)^2
 -\left(\frac\delta2+\frac{\delta^2}{8}\right)n^2.$$ This proves Equation <a href="#eq:two-adic-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:two-adic-bound">[eq:two-adic-bound]</a> and the stated bound for each raw minor. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:odd-primes"></a>
### Odd primes and the finite-place lower bound

Throughout this section assume that $G\in\mathbb Q$, so that the columns $F_j$ of Section <a href="#sec:foundations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:foundations">2</a> are rational. We regard each $F_j$ as a column in $\mathbb Q^n$. For an odd prime $p$, reduction modulo $p$ always means reduction of a member of $\mathbb Z_p$; in particular, every congruence below includes the assertion that its two sides are locally integral.

The raw matrix has $n+q$ columns, whereas the filtered determinant has size $n$. At the large primes treated first, each raw column has at most two powers of $p$ in its denominator. We shall make invertible column changes over $\mathbb Z_p$ and bound how many columns can still have denominator $p^2$ or $p$. Such bounds control every full minor of the raw matrix, and Cauchy–Binet then transfers them to the integer column filter. The first reduction identifies residues after multiplication by $p^2$; for $p>H/2$ a second reduction identifies the remaining residues after multiplication by $p$.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Digit reduction of the rational moments

We first prove the reductions needed at primes $$2\sqrt H<p\le H.$$ We may suppose that $p$ does not divide the denominator of $G$: as $N$ tends to infinity, every fixed denominator prime eventually lies below $2\sqrt H$. Put $$E(t)=(1-t^2)^{(p-1)/2}=\sum_{d=0}^{p-1}E_dt^d
 \quad\text{in }\mathbb F_p[t],\qquad
 \epsilon=(-1)^{(p-1)/2}.$$ We set $E_d=0$ outside $0\le d<p$. Within this range, $$E_d=\begin{cases}c_{d/2}\pmod p,&d\text{ even},\\0,&d\text{ odd},\end{cases}
 \qquad E_{p-1-d}=\epsilon E_d.$$ The first identity follows from $(-1)^k\binom{(p-1)/2}{k}\equiv4^{-k}\binom{2k}{k}$; the second follows by reversing the coefficients of $E$.

<a id="stmt-lem-odd-digit-reductions"></a>
<div id="lem:odd-digit-reductions" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:digit-layers"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Digit reductions). *For $0\le i,j<H$, let $$\ell=j\bmod p,\quad d=(j-i-1)\bmod p,
 \qquad
 i'=\frac{i+1+d-\ell}{p}-1,\quad j'=\left\lfloor\frac jp\right\rfloor,
 \qquad 0\le\ell,d<p.$$ Then $i'\ge-1$, and, with the convention $M^0(-1,j')=k^+_{j'+1}$, $$\label{eq:digit-layers}
 \begin{split}
 p^2M^0(i,j)&\equiv E_dM^0(i',j')\pmod p,\\
 p^2Z^0(i,j)&\equiv
 \begin{cases}
 Z^0(\lfloor i/p\rfloor,j'),&i\equiv j\pmod p,\\
 0,&i\not\equiv j\pmod p.
 \end{cases}
 \end{split}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-odd-digit-reductions"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-odd-digit-reductions</summary>

*Proof.* We first give the parity and carry calculation for the factors $H_z^*$ in the explicit formulas of Section <a href="#sec:foundations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:foundations">2</a>. Write $z=Pp+r$, $0\le r<p$, $0\le z<H$. Since $H<p^2/4$, all factors at the reduced indices $P$ are $p$-adic units. If $z$ is even, then <a id="eq:odd-even-H"></a>$$\label{eq:odd-even-H}
 \begin{cases}
 H_z^*\equiv H_P^*c_{r/2}\pmod p,&r\text{ even},\\
 v_p(H_z^*)=1,&r\text{ odd}.
 \end{cases}$$ Indeed, when $r$ is even, $P$ is even and the base-$p$ digits of $z/2$ are $P/2,r/2$. Expanding $(1+x)^z$ modulo $p$ by $(1+x)^p=1+x^p$ gives the first assertion, including the factor $4^{-z/2}$. When $r$ is odd, $P$ is odd and the low digit of $z/2$ is $(p+r)/2$. The factorial formula for $\binom z{z/2}$ has exactly one carry: $$\left\lfloor\frac zp\right\rfloor
       -2\left\lfloor\frac{z/2}{p}\right\rfloor=1,$$ and there is no contribution from $p^2$. Thus, for positive even $z$, $v_p(zH_z^*)$ is either zero or one. It is one precisely when $r=0$ or $r$ is odd.

<a id="eq:odd-odd-H"></a>
<a id="eq:odd-moment-digit"></a>
For odd $z$ one has $$\label{eq:odd-odd-H}
 \frac1{H_z^*}\in\mathbb Z_p,\qquad
 pH_z^*\equiv
 \begin{cases}
 \epsilon c_{r/2}H_P^*,&r\text{ even},\\
 0,&r\text{ odd}.
 \end{cases}$$ If $r$ is odd, both $z$ and $c_{(z-1)/2}$ are units by the same digit calculation. If $r$ is even, $P$ is odd. At $z=Pp$, digit expansion gives $$c_{(Pp-1)/2}\equiv c_{(P-1)/2}c_{(p-1)/2}
       =\epsilon c_{(P-1)/2},
 \qquad pH_{Pp}^*\equiv\epsilon H_P^*.$$ The recurrence $H_{z+2}^*/H_z^*=(z+1)/(z+2)$, applied through $r=0,2,\ldots,p-1$, now proves the nonzero case of Equation <a href="#eq:odd-odd-H" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:odd-odd-H">[eq:odd-odd-H]</a>. Its denominators on this interval are units. Since $m_{z-1}=2H_z^*$ for odd $z$ and is zero for even $z$, these formulas imply $$\label{eq:odd-moment-digit}
 pm_{z-1}\equiv E_{p-1-r}m_{P-1}\pmod p.$$ This includes $z=0$, using $m_{-1}=0$.

<a id="eq:odd-starting-digits"></a>
We next reduce the two starting arrays. For $u=Pp+r$, $0\le u<H$, we claim $$\label{eq:odd-starting-digits}
 p^2k^-_u\equiv E_{p-1-r}k^-_P,
 \qquad
 p^2k^+_{u+1}\equiv E_r k^+_{P+1}\pmod p.$$ For odd $u$, write the first formula as $$p^2 k^-_u=(pH_u^*)
           \sum_{\substack{1\le z\le u\\z\text{ odd}}}\frac{2p}{z}.$$ Only $z=mp$, with $m$ odd, survives in the sum. If $r$ is odd the first factor vanishes modulo $p$. If $r$ is even, $P$ is odd and Equation <a href="#eq:odd-odd-H" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:odd-odd-H">[eq:odd-odd-H]</a> gives $\epsilon E_rH_P^*\sum_{m\le P,\ m\text{ odd}}2/m
 =E_{p-1-r}k^-_P$, as required.

For even $u$ the quantities $p/(zH_z^*)$, for positive even $z\le u$, are integral. Thus $$p^2k^-_u=H_u^*
     \sum_{\substack{1\le z\le u\\z\text{ even}}}
                  2\left(\frac{p}{zH_z^*}\right)^2$$ is integral, and is zero modulo $p$ when $r$ is odd. If $r$ is even, then $P$ is even. The nonzero summands form precisely the complete blocks $$z=mp-\lambda,\qquad
 m=2,4,\ldots,P,\qquad \lambda=0,2,\ldots,p-1.$$ To see completeness, the indices with $z\bmod p$ odd lie immediately below an even multiple $mp$, while that multiple supplies $\lambda=0$. The upper limit $u=Pp+r$ contains the whole block ending at $Pp$, and the next such block begins at $(P+1)p+1>u$. No partial block is present. The recurrence, started at $mp$ and followed downwards, gives $$\frac{p}{(mp-\lambda)H_{mp-\lambda}^*}
       \equiv\frac{c_{\lambda/2}}{mH_m^*}\pmod p.$$ For example, each downward step from $z$ to $z-2$ multiplies this quantity by $(z-1)/(z-2)$; these multipliers give successively $(2j-1)/(2j)$ modulo $p$. Finally, with $s=(p-1)/2$, $$\sum_{j=0}^{s}c_j^2
  \equiv\sum_{j=0}^{s}\binom{s}{j}^2
  =\binom{2s}{s}\equiv(-1)^s=\epsilon\pmod p.$$ The equality is the coefficient identity obtained from $(1+x)^s(1+x)^s$. Multiplying the block sums by $H_u^*\equiv E_rH_P^*$ proves the first congruence of Equation <a href="#eq:odd-starting-digits" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:odd-starting-digits">[eq:odd-starting-digits]</a> also for even $u$.

For the second congruence, suppose first that $u$ is even. In $$p^2k^+_{u+1}
   =H_u^*\sum_{\substack{1\le z\le u\\z\equiv u\ (2)}}
                     \frac{2p^2}{z^2H_z^*},$$ every term with $p\nmid z$ vanishes modulo $p$, because $v_p(H_z^*)\le1$. At $z=mp$, necessarily $m$ is even and $H_{mp}^*\equiv H_m^*$. The result is $E_r k^+_{P+1}$ when $r$ is even, and zero when $r$ is odd. For odd $u$ instead write the expression as $$(pH_u^*)\sum_{\substack{1\le z\le u\\z\text{ odd}}}
                         \frac{2p}{z^2H_z^*}.$$ The reciprocal $1/H_z^*$ is integral. Again only multiples $z=mp$ can survive; there $pH_{mp}^*\equiv\epsilon H_m^*$, and the two factors of $\epsilon$ cancel. If $r$ is odd the prefactor is zero. This proves the second congruence in every case and also proves the local integrality asserted in Equation <a href="#eq:odd-starting-digits" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:odd-starting-digits">[eq:odd-starting-digits]</a>.

If $i\ge j$, put $u=i-j=Pp+r$. Then $d=p-1-r$ and $i'=j'+P$. The diagonal reduction is $$M^0(i,j)=k^-_u-\sum_{k=1}^{j}\frac{m_{u+k-1}}k.$$ After multiplication by $p^2$, terms with $p\nmid k$ vanish by Equation <a href="#eq:odd-moment-digit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:odd-moment-digit">[eq:odd-moment-digit]</a>. At $k=mp$ the surviving term is $E_{p-1-r}m_{P+m-1}/m$. The first starting congruence therefore gives $$p^2M^0(i,j)\equiv E_{p-1-r}
       \left(k^-_P-\sum_{m=1}^{j'}\frac{m_{P+m-1}}m\right)
       =E_dM^0(i',j').$$ If $i<j$, put $u=j-i-1=Pp+r$, so $d=r$ and $i'=j'-P-1$. Now $$M^0(i,j)=k^+_{u+1}-\sum_{k=u+2}^{j}\frac{m_{k-u-2}}k.$$ For a multiple $k=mp$ in the sum, $$k-u-1=(m-P-1)p+(p-1-r).$$ Equation <a href="#eq:odd-moment-digit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:odd-moment-digit">[eq:odd-moment-digit]</a> gives the reduced summand $E_r m_{m-P-2}/m$. A possible first multiple with $m=P+1$ contributes zero, since its reduced moment is $m_{-1}$. Thus the reduced sum is over $m=P+2,\ldots,j'$, as in the formula for $M^0(i',j')$. The expression for $i'$ is also $i'=\lfloor(i-\ell)/p\rfloor$, so $i'\ge-1$. When $i'=-1$, $j'=P$, the sum is empty and the starting term is exactly $k^+_{j'+1}=M^0(-1,j')$. This proves the first assertion of Equation <a href="#eq:digit-layers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:digit-layers">[eq:digit-layers]</a>, including its boundary convention. The reduced array indices are less than $H/p<p/4$; even the boundary index $j'+1$ is less than $p$. Thus the explicit rational formulas show that all quantities on its right side are integral at $p$.

For $Z^0$, write $I=\lfloor i/p\rfloor$ and $J=\lfloor j/p\rfloor$. Because $i,j<p^2$, the harmonic sums satisfy $$pB_i^{(1)}\equiv B_I^{(1)},\qquad
 p^2B_i^{(2)}\equiv B_I^{(2)}\pmod p.$$ The diagonal formula follows at once. Off the diagonal, if $i\not\equiv j\pmod p$, the denominator $i-j$ is a unit and $p^2Z^0(i,j)$ is zero modulo $p$. If the residues agree, $i-j=p(I-J)$ with $I-J$ a unit; substituting the first harmonic congruence gives $Z^0(I,J)$. This also proves integrality of $p^2Z^0(i,j)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### The leading layer and paired raw columns

<a id="eq:column-layer"></a>
We now gather the moment reductions into column vectors. This will identify pairs of raw columns whose sum has at most one power of $p$ in its denominator. For a fixed residue $0\le\ell<p$, define column vectors over $\mathbb F_p$ by $$P'_u=[t^{(u+1)p+\ell}]\,tP(t)E(t)\quad(u\ge-1),
 \qquad
 D'_u=[t^{up+\ell}]\,D(t)\quad(u\ge0).$$ Here $P,D$ denote the vectors of all row polynomials; the dependence of these extracted vectors on $\ell$ is understood. Their supports are finite. Lemma <a href="#lem:odd-digit-reductions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:odd-digit-reductions">4</a>, gathered by coefficient residue, gives $$\label{eq:column-layer}
 X_{kp+\ell}:=p^2F_{kp+\ell}\bmod p
   =\sum_{u\ge-1}P'_uM^0(u,k)
          -\frac32\sum_{u\ge0}D'_uZ^0(u,k).$$ For a term $[t^i]P$, its unique residue $d$ satisfies $i+1+d=(i'+1)p+\ell$, so that it contributes exactly to $P'_{i'}$, including $i'=-1$. The $Z^0$ reduction survives only when $i=up+\ell$, which is precisely the extraction defining $D'_u$. The coefficient of $G$ in $F_j$ is a sum of integral multiples of $c_{(i-j)/2}$. These coefficients are integral at every odd prime, so this term disappears after multiplication by $p^2$. Every raw column therefore lies in $p^{-2}\mathbb Z_p^n$.

<a id="eq:odd-paired-layer"></a>
If $\ell\ge H-2p$, the degree bounds $\deg(tPE)\le H+p-1$ and $\deg D<H$ show that $P'_u,D'_u$ vanish for $u>1$. Put $$V_\ell=2P'_1-\frac32D'_1,
 \qquad U_\ell=2P'_{-1}-\frac32D'_0.$$ The needed small values of the reduced arrays are $$\begin{array}{c|rrr}
 u&-1&0&1\\\hline
 M^0(u,0)&0&0&2\\
 M^0(u,1)&2&0&-2
 \end{array}
 \qquad
 \begin{array}{c|rr}
 u&0&1\\\hline
 Z^0(u,0)&0&1\\
 Z^0(u,1)&1&-1
 \end{array}.$$ Consequently, whenever the indicated columns exist, $$\label{eq:odd-paired-layer}
 X_\ell=V_\ell,\qquad X_{p+\ell}=U_\ell-V_\ell.$$ Since $tP$ and $D$ have no terms below degree $A+1$, $U_\ell=0$ for $\ell\le A$.

We record explicitly how changes in the full column pool will be used. Let $T$ be the $n$ by $(n+q)$ matrix of all raw columns. If $Q\in\operatorname{GL}_{n+q}(\mathbb Z_p)$ and every full minor of $TQ$ has valuation at least $-D$, the same holds for every full minor of $T=(TQ)Q^{-1}$ by Cauchy–Binet. The coefficients in this expansion are minors of the integral matrix $Q^{-1}$. Applying Cauchy–Binet once more to the integer matrix defining the prescribed filter gives $v_p(\Delta_N)\ge-D$. This transfer applies even when the columns or their leading residues are linearly dependent.

<a id="eq:odd-lower-pair-range"></a>
For $2\sqrt H<p\le H/2$, use each pair of columns with $$\max(b,H-2p)\le\ell<\min(p,L-p,A).$$ Their number is exactly $$d_0=\bigl(\min(p,L-p,A)-\max(b,H-2p)\bigr)_+,
 \qquad y_+=\max(y,0).$$ Replacing its high column by the sum of the pair is an integral elementary column operation with integral inverse. The pair sum lies in $p^{-1}\mathbb Z_p^n$ by Equation <a href="#eq:odd-paired-layer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:odd-paired-layer">[eq:odd-paired-layer]</a>; all other columns still lie in $p^{-2}\mathbb Z_p^n$. These pairs are disjoint, since $\ell<p$. Any selection of $n$ columns from the pool of $n+q$ contains at least $(d_0-q)_+$ of the improved columns. The transfer just explained gives $$\label{eq:odd-lower-pair-range}
 v_p(\Delta_N)\ge-2n+(d_0-q)_+.$$

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The central layer and integral column elimination

For $p>H/2$, the next lemma identifies the residues of central columns after multiplication by $p$. It expresses them in terms of the same vectors $V_\ell$ that occur in the leading residues of noncentral columns. The elimination proof will retain noncentral columns and use $p$ times those columns to cancel the corresponding $V_\ell$ terms.

<a id="stmt-lem-odd-central-layer"></a>
<div id="lem:odd-central-layer" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:central-layer"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Central layer). *Suppose $H/2<p\le H$, and write $K=L-p$ and $J=H-p$. For every central column, by which we mean $$b\le j<\min(p,L),\qquad j\ge J,$$ one has $$\label{eq:central-layer}
 pF_j\in\mathbb Z_p^n,\qquad
 pF_j\equiv-\sum_{0\le\ell<J}\frac{V_\ell}{j-\ell}\pmod p.$$ All denominators $j-\ell$ occurring here are units at $p$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-odd-central-layer"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-odd-central-layer</summary>

*Proof.* For every contributing row degree $0\le i<H$, $i-j\le H-1-J=p-1$ and $j-i<p$. Hence $|i-j|<p$, and the starting value $k^-_{i-j}$ or $k^+_{j-i}$ in the diagonal reduction is integral at $p$. In either case that reduction can be written as the starting value minus $$\sum_{0\le\ell<\min(i,j)}\frac{m_{i-\ell-1}}{j-\ell}.$$ Here $1\le j-\ell<p$. By Equation <a href="#eq:odd-moment-digit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:odd-moment-digit">[eq:odd-moment-digit]</a>, a nonzero reduction after multiplication by $p$ requires $p\le i-\ell<2p$, and then $$pm_{i-\ell-1}\equiv2E_{2p+\ell-1-i}.$$ Such an index necessarily has $0\le\ell<J$. Conversely its coefficient can be collected over all $i$ with no truncation, since $j\ge J>\ell$ and $i\ge p+\ell>\ell$. Thus $$pM^0(P,j)\equiv-\sum_{0\le\ell<J}\frac{2P'_1}{j-\ell}.$$ The extracted vector $P'_1$ in each summand is the one belonging to that residue $\ell$. For $Z^0(i,j)$, the harmonic sums have a pole only if $i=p+\ell$ with $0\le\ell<J$. The denominator $i-j$ is then a unit: equality modulo $p$ would require $j=\ell<J$. Therefore $$pZ^0(p+\ell,j)\equiv\frac1{\ell-j}.$$ All other $Z^0(i,j)$ are integral. Combining these two calculations with the factor $-3/2$ proves Equation <a href="#eq:central-layer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:central-layer">[eq:central-layer]</a>; the $G$ term is integral here as before. ◻

</details>

We now carry out these cancellations in the full column pool. In the resulting pool, $R$ will count the retained columns with possible denominator $p^2$, and $S$ will bound the number of other columns with possible denominator $p$; every remaining column will be integral. The proof first uses the $V_\ell$ supplied by retained noncentral columns to modify the central columns. We then use the rank of their remaining scaled residues to bound the number of central columns that can still have denominator $p$ after an invertible column change.

<a id="stmt-lem-odd-local-elimination"></a>
<div id="lem:odd-local-elimination" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:odd-two-layer-bound"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Elimination over the local integers). *For $H/2<p\le H$, define $$\begin{aligned}
 R&=K_++(K-A)_++(J-\max(b,K))_+,\\
 S&=(\min(A,K)-b)_+
        +(\min(b,J)-\max(0,K))_+.
\end{aligned}$$ Then every full raw minor, and hence the filtered determinant, satisfies $$\label{eq:odd-two-layer-bound}
 v_p(\Delta_N)\ge-\min(2n,n+R,2R+S).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-odd-local-elimination"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-odd-local-elimination</summary>

*Proof.* The noncentral columns are precisely the high columns $F_{p+\ell}$ for $0\le\ell<K$ and the low columns $F_\ell$ for $b\le\ell<J$. Empty ranges are allowed. Indeed $p>b$, $J<p$, and $K<J$. For $$b\le\ell<\min(A,K)$$ retain the low column and replace the high column by $F_{p+\ell}+F_\ell$. Let $$B=(\min(A,K)-b)_+$$ be the number of these pair sums. Each lies in $p^{-1}\mathbb Z_p^n$. Retain every other noncentral column as a column in $p^{-2}\mathbb Z_p^n$, whether or not its leading residue depends on the others. The number of columns so retained is <a id="eq:odd-retained-count"></a>$$\label{eq:odd-retained-count}
 R_0=K_++(J-b)_+-B.$$ It equals the stated $R$. To verify the identity, split $K$ into $K\le0$, $0<K\le b$, $b<K\le A$, and $K>A$; use $K<J$ in the last two cases. The resulting formulas are respectively $(J-b)_+$, $K+(J-b)_+$, $J$, and $K+J-A$.

These retained columns furnish a representative of every vector $V_\ell$ with $0\le\ell<J$ except possibly those in $$\mathcal E=\{\ell:\max(0,K)\le\ell<\min(b,J)\}.$$ For $b\le\ell<J$, the retained low column has leading residue $p^2F_\ell\equiv V_\ell$. For $0\le\ell<\min(b,K)$, the unpaired high column has leading residue $-V_\ell$, because $\ell<b<A$ makes $U_\ell=0$. These two ranges exhaust the complement of $\mathcal E$. Denote the size of $\mathcal E$ by $$e=|\mathcal E|=(\min(b,J)-\max(0,K))_+.$$

For each represented residue choose a retained column $Y_\ell$ and a sign $\sigma_\ell\in\{1,-1\}$ such that $p^2Y_\ell\equiv\sigma_\ell V_\ell$. For every central $j$, choose $a_{\ell j}\in\mathbb Z_p$ reducing to $\sigma_\ell/(j-\ell)$ and replace that column by $$C_j=F_j+p\sum_{\substack{0\le\ell<J\\\ell\notin\mathcal E}}
                                      a_{\ell j}Y_\ell.$$ This is a shear of the whole column pool with integral coefficients and inverse obtained by changing the signs of the added coefficients. It preserves every retained column and every pair sum. The resulting central columns lie in $p^{-1}\mathbb Z_p^n$ and satisfy $$pC_j\equiv-\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal E}\frac{V_\ell}{j-\ell}
       \pmod p.$$ This step does not require any information about the next coefficient of $Y_\ell$. Explicitly, if $Y_\ell=p^{-2}y_0+p^{-1}y_1+y_2$ with integral lifts $y_0,y_1,y_2$, then $pY_\ell=p^{-1}y_0+y_1+py_2$; its unknown second coefficient is already integral.

Let $c$ be the number of central columns. Their scaled residues define a linear map $$\varphi:\mathbb F_p^c\longrightarrow\mathbb F_p^n,
 \qquad (a_j)_j\longmapsto\sum_j a_j(pC_j\bmod p).$$ Its image is contained in the span of the $e$ exceptional vectors, so $d:=\operatorname{rank}\varphi\le\min(c,e)$. Choose a basis of $\mathbb F_p^c$ whose last $c-d$ vectors form a basis of $\ker\varphi$. Lift its basis matrix arbitrarily to a matrix $W\in\operatorname{Mat}_{c}(\mathbb Z_p)$. Its determinant is a unit, so $W\in\operatorname{GL}_c(\mathbb Z_p)$ and the adjugate formula gives $W^{-1}\in\operatorname{Mat}_{c}(\mathbb Z_p)$. Applying this change to the central columns leaves at most $d$ columns in $p^{-1}\mathbb Z_p^n$; the other $c-d$ are integral, because their scaled residues vanish and they already belonged to $p^{-1}\mathbb Z_p^n$. If $c=0$ this step is the empty identity.

The resulting full pool consequently contains $R$ columns with possible denominator $p^2$, at most $B+d\le B+e=S$ other columns with possible denominator $p$, and integral remaining columns. No noncentral column was discarded, nor was its denominator reduced on the strength of a leading linear dependence. In a full minor, if $r$ columns come from the first group and $s$ from the second, the loss is at most $2r+s$. The inequalities $$2r+s\le2n,\qquad 2r+s\le n+R,\qquad 2r+s\le2R+S$$ hold since $r+s\le n$, $r\le R$, and $s\le S$. All changes used above and their inverses are integral, so the Cauchy–Binet transfer proves Equation <a href="#eq:odd-two-layer-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:odd-two-layer-bound">[eq:odd-two-layer-bound]</a> for all original full minors and for $\Delta_N$. ◻

</details>

The omission of the optional pair at $\ell=A$ is harmless: its physical column is included in $R$. The preceding proof also covers $K\le0$ and $J\le b$. In particular, if $L\le p\le H$, there are no high or low noncentral columns, $R=0$, and $S=H-p$; all raw columns are central. At the formal endpoint $p=H$, all of them are integral.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Summing the prime losses
